How certain are you about that?\n\nPatient: Even though my family and friends tell me I look fine and don't notice anything, it's really hard to believe them because the flaws on my nose and skin feel so real and obvious to me. I feel completely certain that I look terrible, which is why I end up spending hours zooming in on photos and picking myself apart.\n\nDoctor: Has there ever been any moment when you could consider that your nose and skin might not actually look as defective as they seem to you, or are you completely convinced that other people can clearly see these flaws? Do you ever think your mind might be exaggerating them at all?\n\nPatient: It is incredibly hard to believe people like my mom or roommate when they tell me I look fine, because the flaws feel so obvious and huge to me. When I look in the mirror or at photos, my crooked nose and uneven skin completely consume me, and I'm convinced that's what everyone else is seeing too. That's why I keep asking for reassurance, even though their answers never actually make me feel any better."

Or did you still feel completely certain something was seriously wrong with how you look?\n\nPatient: I haven't actually seen a doctor or a dermatologist about my nose or skin, so I'm not really sure how to answer that part. But even though other people keep telling me I look fine, I still feel completely certain that my nose is crooked and my skin is uneven.\n\nDoctor: Because of these concerns about your nose and skin, do you spend a lot of time doing things like checking mirrors, comparing yourself with other people, picking at your skin, or trying to hide the areas? About how many hours a day does this take?\n\nPatient: Yes, I easily spend hours every day on these behaviors. I take dozens of selfies to check angles, constantly redo my makeup to hide my nose and skin, and change outfits three or four times before work. I also spend hours zooming in on photos to pick apart my flaws and constantly ask my mom or roommate for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your nose and skin problems are definitely real and visible to others, or is there any part of you that thinks these worries might be exaggerated by your mind? Have you ever sought cosmetic treatment or avoided it because others disagreed with you?"
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
To me, the flaws on my nose and skin feel completely real and obvious, and I can't help but fixate on them even though everyone tells me they don't notice anything. I haven't actually sought out any professional cosmetic treatments, but I spend a massive amount of effort trying to camouflage these flaws myself using makeup, clothing, and camera angles.

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
